Risk Phase Transitions in Spiked Regression: Alignment Driven Benign and Catastrophic Overfitting
Jiping Li, Rishi Sonthalia

Abstract
This paper analyzes the generalization error of minimum-norm interpolating solutions in linear regression using spiked covariance data models. The paper characterizes how varying spike strengths and target-spike alignments can affect risk, especially in overparameterized settings. The study presents an exact expression for the generalization error, leading to a comprehensive classification of benign, tempered, and catastrophic overfitting regimes based on spike strength, the aspect ratio c = d / n c=d/n (particularly as c → ∞ c\to\infty ), and target alignment. Notably, in well-specified aligned problems, increasing spike strength can surprisingly induce catastrophic overfitting before achieving benign overfitting. The paper also reveals that target-spike alignment is not always advantageous, identifying specific, sometimes counterintuitive, conditions for its benefit or detriment. Alignment with the spike being detrimental is empirically demonstrated to persist in nonlinear models.



1
Introduction

Understanding the generalization error of overparameterized models is a central challenge in modern machine learning. Phenomena such as double descent
(
belkin2019reconciling
;
hastie2022surprises
)
and benign overfitting
bartlett2020benign
;
mallinar2022benign
;
tsigler2023benign
have spurred research underscoring the critical role of the data’s spectral structure
bartlett2020benign
;
dobriban2018high
;
hastie2022surprises
;
kausik2024double
;
mei2022generalization
;
sonthalia2023training
;
tsigler2023benign
;
wang2024near
. The spiked covariance model is one commonly considered spectral structure
couillet_liao_2022
. In this model, the data matrix
{\bm{X}}={\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
, comprising
n
data points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, is decomposed into a rank-one signal component (“spike”)
{\bm{Z}}
and an isotropic noise component (“bulk”)
{\bm{A}}
. Spiked covariance models emerge naturally in practice, for instance, in the features learned by neural networks during training
sonthalia2025low
;
ba2022high
;
ba2023learning
;
damian2022neural
;
dandi2024how
;
martin2021implicit
;
moniri2023theory
;
wang2024nonlinear
. While recent studies have examined benign overfitting in spiked models
(
ba2023learning
;
kausik2024double
)
, they lack a systematic taxonomy spanning spike strength, target–spike alignment, model misspecification, and train–test covariate shift. This paper closes the gap for linear regression.

This work explores how general spike sizes and target alignments affect generalization error in least squares linear regression. We consider targets
{\bm{y}}
generated by:

{\bm{y}}=\alpha_{Z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{z}}+\alpha_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{a}}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}

Here,
{\bm{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
represents the signal component,
{\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
corresponds to the bulk component,
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
is observation noise, and
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The coefficients
\alpha_{Z}
and
\alpha_{A}
model the target’s dependence on the spike and bulk components, respectively. Notably, if
\alpha_{A}\neq\alpha_{Z}
, the targets are non-linear functions of
{\bm{x}}={\bm{z}}+{\bm{a}}
, introducing model mis-specification. We address two fundamental questions:

•

Q1:
For a fixed aspect ratio
c=d/n
, in asympototic proportional regime under what conditions does alignment of the target signal with the data spike improve or impair generalization?

•

Q2:
In the high-dimensional limit where
c\to\infty
, when do we observe benign, tempered, or catastrophic overfitting regimes?

Contributions
We present precise characterization of the generalization performance of minimum-norm interpolating solutions in linear regression. Our exact risk decomposition pinpoints conditions for transitions between benign and catastrophic overfitting. This reveals alignment-dependent phenomena obscured by isotropic theories, clarifying how signal structure, data scaling, and overparameterization shape generalization. Our primary contributions are as follows:

•

Precise Risk Characterization:
We derive an exact generalization error decomposition (
Theorem

5
) into interpretable bias, variance, data noise, and alignment terms.

•

Comprehensive Categorization of Overfitting Regimes:
We precisely classify benign, tempered, or catastrophic overfitting regimes based on spike strength, overparameterization (
c=d/n
), and target alignment (Table
1
).
Surprisingly, for well-specified aligned problems, increasing spike strength can induce catastrophic overfitting before achieving benign overfitting. Misspecified problems show distinct transitions, often precluding benign overfitting.

•

Conditions for Beneficial Alignment:
Challenging conventional wisdom, we show spike alignment is not always beneficial and depends on spike strength meeting critical thresholds (Table
2
).
For misspecified problems, beneficial alignment requires
\alpha_{Z}/\alpha_{A}
in a specific, non-trivial range. Counterintuitively, very strong spike dependence (
\alpha_{Z}/\alpha_{A}
) can render alignment detrimental.

•

Empirical Validation:
1
1

1

Our code is available at the anonymous GitHub repository:
link
Empirical validation confirms our theoretical phenomena, including surprising negative alignment impacts, persist in nonlinear models, underscoring broader relevance.

Benign Overfitting in Linear Regression.

Significant research has explored benign overfitting in linear regression
bartlett2020benign
;
cao2021risk
;
JMLR:v22:20-974
;
karhadkar2024benign
;
koehler2021uniform
;
liang2020just
;
mallinar2022benign
;
muthukumar2020harmless
;
shamir2022implicit
;
tsigler2023benign
;
wu2020optimal
. Many studies assume a uniformly bounded largest covariance eigenvalue or lack precise characterizations of its interplay with target alignment and generalization.
Our work allows this eigenvalue to grow, offering precise performance characterizations based on this growth and alignment.
While
kausik2024double
considers spiked models, their focus is on noiseless, well-specified scenarios with specific spike scaling.
Our analysis is broader, encompassing observation noise, misspecification, and general spike scaling.

Many prior works
(
karhadkar2024benign
;
shamir2022implicit
;
tsigler2023benign
)
on benign overfitting with low-rank signals plus isotropic noise require near-orthogonality between signal and noise, sometimes imposing strong conditions like
d=\Omega(n^{2}\log n)
.
We instead consider the proportional regime
d/n\to c=\Theta(1)
, subsequently examining
c\to\infty
.
This setting is morally similar to allowing
d=\omega(n)
and aligns with approaches like
(
karhadkar2024benign
)
which, for classification, shows misclassification probability can be upper bounded by
Ce^{-d/n}
, vanishing as
d/n\to\infty
.

Generalization Error with Spiked Covariance.

While recovering spike properties
sonthalia2023training
;
kausik2024double
;
nadakuditi2014optshrink
;
benaych2011eigenvalues
;
benaych2012singular
and analyzing generalization error in spiked models
ba2022high
;
ba2023learning
;
mousavi2023gradient
;
moniri2023theory
are active research areas, existing analyses often characterize generalization implicitly (e.g., via fixed-point equations) or focus on specific spike strengths/alignments.
In contrast, we provide explicit, generic formulae for generalization error, enabling precise categorization of overfitting regimes and conditions for beneficial spike alignment.

Notation

The subscript on
o,O,\omega,\Omega,\Theta
will denote which quantity is being sent to infinity.

2
Problem Setting

We study the generalization of minimum-norm interpolators in high-dimensional linear regression. Using a spiked covariance data model, we quantify how spike strength and alignment influence generalization and the emergence of benign, tempered, or catastrophic overfitting.

Data Model.

We consider a data matrix
{\bm{X}}={\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
with
signal component

{\bm{Z}}
and
isotropic noise component

{\bm{A}}
that satisfy the following assumptions. Specifically, we shall that the population feature covariance is
{\bm{\Sigma}}=\theta^{2}{\bm{u}}{\bm{u}}^{\top}+\tau^{2}{\bm{I}}_{d}
, modeling a rank-one perturbation of isotropic noise.

Assumption 1

(Signal)
.

Let
{\bm{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a fixed unit vector representing the spike direction. Then

{\bm{Z}}=\theta{\bm{u}}{\bm{v}}^{\top},

(1)

where
\theta>0
controls the spike strength, and the vector
{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has i.i.d. standard normal entries.

Assumption 2

(Noise)
.

The entries of
{\bm{A}}
have zero mean and variance
\tau^{2}
. The matrix
{\bm{A}}
satisfies:

•

Its entries are uncorrelated and possess finite fourth moments.

•

Its distribution is invariant under left and right orthogonal transformations.

•

The empirical spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{\tau^{2}d}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\top}
converges to the Marchenko–Pastur law as
n,d\to\infty
with
d/n\to c\in(0,\infty)
.

Spike Strength Normalizations.

We consider two key scaling regimes for the spike strength relative to the bulk noise. These lead to distinct generalization behaviors.

1.

Operator Norm Scaling (
\theta^{2}=\gamma\tau^{2}
):

Here
\gamma
tunes the spike strength
\theta^{2}
relative to the noise variance
\tau^{2}
. When
\gamma=(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
, the spectral norm of the signal component
{\bm{Z}}
is comparable to that of the noise component
{\bm{A}}
. If
\gamma>(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
, the spike emerges as an isolated eigenvalue beyond the bulk spectrum established by
{\bm{A}}
, a phenomenon known as the Baik–Ben Arous–Péché (BBP) transition
(
baik2005phase
)
. This scaling reflects spikes in learned neural network features
(
ba2022high
;
moniri2023theory
)
.

2.

Frobenius Norm Scaling (
\theta^{2}=d\tau^{2}
):

Here
\theta^{2}=d\tau^{2}
matches expected signal and noise Frobenius norms (
\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{Z}}\|_{F}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{A}}\|_{F}^{2}]
) and the spike has macroscopic proportion of the energy. Such strong signals can lead to improved sample complexity, potentially overcoming limitations observed in purely isotropic models
(
ba2023learning
;
mei2022generalization
)
.

Target Model.

Given
{\bm{x}}_{i}={\bm{z}}_{i}+{\bm{a}}_{i}
, the targets
{\bm{y}}
are obtained as follows:

{\bm{y}}_{i}=\alpha_{Z}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\alpha_{A}{\bm{a}}_{i}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i},

(2)

where
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in uniformly distributed in the subspace
\{{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}:{\bm{\beta}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}=\text{ fixed constant}\}
is the true underlying parameter vector. The terms
{\bm{z}}_{i}
and
{\bm{a}}_{i}
are the
i
-th columns of
{\bm{Z}}
and
{\bm{A}}
respectively. The observation noise
{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}
are i.i.d. with
\mathbb{E}[{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{i}^{2}]=\tau_{\varepsilon}^{2}
. The coefficients
\alpha_{Z},\alpha_{A}\in\mathbb{R}
control the target’s dependence on the signal and noise components. If
\alpha_{Z}\neq\alpha_{A}
, the true data generating process for
{\bm{y}}
differentially weights components of
x_{i}
, causing model misspecification.

Generalization Risk.

We study the minimum-norm interpolating ordinary least squares estimator:

{\bm{\beta}}_{int}={\bm{X}}^{\dagger}{\bm{y}},\qquad\text{ with }\qquad\hat{{\bm{y}}}=(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}+\tilde{{\bm{a}}}){\bm{\beta}}_{int}

(3)

where
{\bm{X}}^{\dagger}
denotes the pseudoinverse. Given a new test data point
(\tilde{{\bm{x}}},\tilde{{\bm{y}}})
, where
\tilde{{\bm{x}}}=\tilde{{\bm{z}}}+\tilde{{\bm{a}}}
and targets
\tilde{{\bm{y}}}=\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}\tilde{{\bm{z}}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\tilde{\alpha}_{A}\tilde{{\bm{a}}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\tilde{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}
with potentially with different coefficients
\tilde{\alpha}_{Z},\tilde{\alpha}_{A}
and model parameters
\tilde{\tau},\tilde{\tau_{\varepsilon}}
, the generalization risk is defined as the expected squared prediction error:

\mathcal{R}({\bm{\beta}}_{int})=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{X}},{\bm{\varepsilon}},\{\tilde{{\bm{x}}},\tilde{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}\}}\left[(\tilde{{\bm{y}}}-\hat{{\bm{y}}})^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{X}},{\bm{\varepsilon}},\{\tilde{{\bm{x}}},\tilde{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}\}}\left[(\tilde{{\bm{y}}}-\tilde{{\bm{x}}}^{T}{\bm{\beta}}_{int})^{2}\right].

(4)

The expectation is over the training data
({\bm{X}},{\bm{\varepsilon}})
and the test data realization
(\{\tilde{{\bm{x}}},\tilde{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}\})
. We shall denote the asymptotic excess risk in the proportional regime as follows:

\mathcal{R}_{c}=\lim_{n,d\to\infty,\,d/n\to c}\mathcal{R}({\bm{\beta}}_{int})-\tilde{\tau}^{2}_{\varepsilon}.

Remark 1

(Generalizing Prior Work)
.

This problem formulation encompasses several existing models as special cases. For instance, isotropic regression settings studied in
hastie2022surprises
are recovered by setting
\theta=0
(no spike) and
\alpha_{Z}=0
. Spike recovery models, such as in
sonthalia2023training
, correspond to specific choices like
\tau^{2}=1/d
,
\tau_{\varepsilon}^{2}=0
, and
\alpha_{A}=0
. Our generalized setup allows for a nuanced investigation of the interplay between signal structure, target alignment, and overparameterization.

Quantifying the Benefit of Alignment.

A key aspect of our investigation is to determine when the alignment of the true parameter vector
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
with the data’s principal spike direction
{\bm{u}}
is beneficial for generalization. We define alignment as
beneficial
if the generalization risk
\mathcal{R}({\bm{\beta}}_{int})
(or
\mathcal{R}_{c}
), is monotonically decreasing as a function of
({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\in[0,1]
. Conversely, alignment is
detrimental
if the risk is a monotonically increasing function of
({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}
.

Characterizing Overfitting Regimes.

Following
bartlett2020benign
;
mallinar2022benign
, we classify the asymptotic behavior of the excess risk,
\mathcal{R}_{c}
as
c\to\infty
as benign, tempered or catastrophic.
We say the overfitting is
benign
if
\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{c}
is zero,
tempered
if this limit is positive and finite,
catastrophic
if this limit is infinite.

3
Theoretical Results

Our core theoretical contribution is a precise analytical formula for excess risk in the spiked covariance model. This result relies on Assumption
3
, which encompasses both the operator norm scaling
(
\theta^{2}=\gamma\tau^{2}
) and Frobenius norm scaling (
\theta^{2}=d\tau^{2}
)
regimes. We develop our general risk theorem by analyzing progressively complex scenarios. Specifically, our forthcoming theorems provide specific conditions for benign, tempered, or catastrophic overfitting (as
c\to\infty
), and determine when, for finite
c
, alignment of
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
with spike

{\bm{u}}
is beneficial or detrimental.

Assumption 3

(Scaling)
.

As
n,d\to\infty
with
d/n\to c\in(0,\infty)
, we assume that

\theta^{2}
and
\tau^{2}
satisfy
\Omega(\tau^{2})\leq\theta^{2}\leq O(d\tau^{2})
and
\tau^{2}=\Theta(1)
.

3.1
Well Specified Problem

(a)

Operator norm scaling (
\theta^{2}=c\tau^{2}
). Alignment initially improves generalization, but have catastrophic risk as
c\to\infty
. Anti-alignment yields tempered risk.

(b)

Equal Frobenius norm scaling (
\theta^{2}=d\tau^{2}
). Alignment leads to benign overfitting, while anti-alignment results in tempered risk.

Figure 1
:
Excess error vs. overparameterization ratio
c=d/n
in the well-specified case. Each plot shows the risk for aligned and anti-aligned targets under different spike scaling regimes.
The scatter plots are empirically obtained and the lines are theory.

We begin by analyzing the well-specified case, where the target
{\bm{y}}
is a direct linear function of the observed covariates
{\bm{X}}={\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}}
. This scenario is realized by setting:

\alpha_{Z}=\alpha_{A}=\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}=\tilde{\alpha}_{A}=\alpha>0.

Consequently,
y_{i}=\alpha{\bm{x}}_{i}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\varepsilon_{i}
, and the model is properly specified.

Theorem 1

(Well-Specified Risk)
.

Given data
({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})
and
(\tilde{{\bm{X}}},\tilde{{\bm{y}}})
generated according to Assumptions
1
(Signal),
2
(Noise), Equation
2
(Target Model), and Assumption
3
(Scaling). If the well-specification condition
\alpha_{Z}=\alpha_{A}=\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}=\tilde{\alpha}_{A}=\alpha>0
holds, the asymptotic excess risk
\mathcal{R}_{c}
is:

\mathcal{R}_{c}=\begin{cases}\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\frac{c}{1-c}&\text{if }c<1\\
\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{c-1}+\alpha^{2}\tau^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{c}\right)\left[\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}+({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\,\frac{\theta^{2}\tau^{2}c^{2}-2\theta^{2}\tau^{2}c-\theta^{4}}{(\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c)^{2}}\right]&\text{if }c>1\end{cases}

where
{\bm{u}}
is the unit vector defining the spike direction.

Remark 2
.

If
\theta^{2}=\gamma\tau^{2}
with
\gamma=o(1)
(a regime not allowed by Assumption
3
but useful for sanity checks), the coefficient of
({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}
vanishes, the risk expression aligns with that of isotropic models, such as in
(
hastie2022surprises
, Theorem 1)
.

Operator Norm Scaling (
\theta^{2}=\gamma\tau^{2}
).

In this regime, the excess risk for
c>1
becomes:

\mathcal{R}_{c}=\alpha^{2}\tau^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{c}\right)\left(\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}+\frac{\gamma c^{2}-2\gamma c-\gamma^{2}}{(\gamma+c)^{2}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\right)+\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{1}{c-1}.

The formula shows that alignment with the spike direction
{\bm{u}}
is beneficial if and only if the coefficient of
({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}
is negative, which occurs when
\gamma>c(c-2)
. We consider different scalings for
\gamma
.

Case 1:
\gamma=\Theta_{c}(1)
(constant with respect to
c
).

The condition for beneficial alignment,
\gamma>c(c-2)
, interacts intricately with the BBP phase transition condition,
\gamma>(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
. Let
c_{*}\approx 4.212
be the unique solution to
c(c-2)=(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
for
c>1
.

•

For
1<c<c_{*}
: Here,
c(c-2)<(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
. If
c(c-2)<\gamma<(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
, alignment is beneficial even though the BBP transition has
not
occurred (the spike is not resolved from the bulk).

•

For
c>c_{*}
: Here,
c(c-2)>(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
. For alignment to be beneficial (
\gamma>c(c-2)
), the BBP transition must have occurred (as
\gamma>c(c-2)\implies\gamma>(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
). However, the BBP transition occurring is not sufficient for beneficial alignment. If
(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}<\gamma<c(c-2)
, the BBP transition occurs, yet alignment is detrimental.

Regarding the type of overfitting as
c\to\infty
(while
\gamma
remains constant):

\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{c}=\alpha^{2}\,\tau^{2}\left(\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}+\gamma({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\right).

Since this limit is a positive constant, we consistently observe
tempered overfitting
when
\gamma=\Theta_{c}(1)
.

Case 2:
\gamma=\omega_{c}(1)
(
\gamma
grows with
c
).

The behavior depends on the growth rate of
\gamma
relative to
c
. The limit of the excess risk for
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\neq 0
as
c\to\infty
is:

\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{c}=\alpha^{2}\tau^{2}\cdot\begin{cases}\infty&\text{if }\omega_{c}(1)\leq\gamma\leq o_{c}(c^{2})\\
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}+(\frac{1}{\phi}-1)({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}&\text{if }\gamma=\phi c^{2}\text{ for const. }\phi>0\\
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}-({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}&\text{if }\gamma=\omega_{c}(c^{2})\end{cases}

Surprisingly, while
\gamma=\Theta_{c}(1)
gives tempered overfitting, increasing spike strength to
\omega_{c}(1)\leq\gamma\leq o_{c}(c^{2})
results in
catastrophic overfitting
, even though morally, this version of the problem has less noise. Additionally, we see that this catastrophic overfitting is not present in the anti-aligned (
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})
case. More, aligned with intuition, we see that further increasing the size of the spike improves the generalization performance. Specifically, we get
tempered overfitting
if
\gamma=\phi c^{2}
and
benign overfitting
if
\gamma=\omega_{c}(c^{2})
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\parallel{\bm{u}}
and
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|=1
.

For
\gamma=c
, the
({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}
coefficient is
(c-3)/4
.
Thus, for
1<c<3
, alignment is beneficial and for
c>3
, alignment becomes detrimental. As
c\to\infty
, if
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\parallel{\bm{u}}
, the excess risk grows approximately as
\alpha^{2}\tau^{2}\frac{c}{4}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}
, indicating
catastrophic overfitting
. In contrast, if
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\perp{\bm{u}}
, the excess risk grows like
\alpha^{2}\tau^{2}(1-1/c)\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}
, leading to
tempered overfitting
. This transition is illustrated in
Figure

1(a)
.

Frobenius Norm Scaling (
\theta^{2}=d\tau^{2}
).

The excess risk for
c>1
simplifies to:

\mathcal{R}_{c>1}=\alpha^{2}\tau^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{c}\right)\left(\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}-({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\right)+\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{1}{c-1}.

We have a few observations. First, if
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\parallel{\bm{u}}
and
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|=1
, the excess risk
\mathcal{R}_{c}
tends to
0
as
c\to\infty
(
benign overfitting
). Second, if
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
is not perfectly aligned with
{\bm{u}}
,
\mathcal{R}_{c}\to\alpha^{2}\tau^{2}(\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}-({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2})>0
as
c\to\infty
(
tempered overfitting
). Finally, the coefficient of
({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}
in the risk formula is negative. Hence, in contrast with the operator norm regime,
alignment is always beneficial
in this regime for
c>1
, and we visualize these behaviors in
Figure

1(b)
.

Takeaways for the Well-Specified Case.

Spike scaling profoundly impacts overfitting, especially with target alignment. For aligned targets, increasing spike strength can drive transitions from tempered
\rightarrow
catastrophic
\rightarrow
tempered
\rightarrow
benign overfitting, while anti-alignment (
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\perp{\bm{u}}
) can mitigate catastrophic overfitting. Additionally, alignment with the spike is not always beneficial.

3.2
Misspecified Case and no Covariate Shift

We next consider misspecified targets
{\bm{y}}
with differing dependence on spike
{\bm{Z}}
and noise
{\bm{A}}
feature components. Specifically, we assume
\alpha_{Z}\neq\alpha_{A}
but introduce no covariate shift between training and test distributions, i.e.,
\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}=\alpha_{Z}
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{A}=\alpha_{A}
.
This scenario models situations where intrinsic feature properties lead to differential correlations with the target, a common occurrence in practice.
For notational convenience, we define
\Delta_{c}:=\alpha_{Z}-\frac{\alpha_{A}}{c}
with
\Delta_{1}:=\alpha_{Z}-\alpha_{A}
.

Theorem 2

(Misspecified)
.

Let
{\bm{Z}},\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}
satisfy
1
,
{\bm{A}},\tilde{{\bm{A}}}
satisfy
2
and
{\bm{y}},\tilde{{\bm{y}}}
according to
Equation

2
. If
3
holds with
\alpha_{Z}=\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}
,
\alpha_{A}=\tilde{\alpha}_{A}
, then

\mathcal{R}_{c}=\begin{cases}\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\frac{c}{1-c}+\tau^{2}\,({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\,\frac{\Delta_{1}^{2}}{1-c}\,\frac{\theta^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}}&c<1\\[10.0pt]
\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{c-1}+\alpha_{A}^{2}\tau^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{c}\right)+\tau^{2}\,({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\,\Delta_{c}^{2}\,\frac{\theta^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c}\left[\frac{c}{c-1}\,\frac{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c}-2\frac{\alpha_{A}}{\Delta_{c}}\right]&c>1\end{cases}

A key observation is that misspecification (
\alpha_{Z}\neq\alpha_{A}
) can itself induce double descent, even if
\tau_{\varepsilon}^{2}=0
. This contrasts with the well-specified case where, if
\tau_{\varepsilon}^{2}=0
, double descent is absent. However, in the misspecified case, we do not observe double descent if there is no alignment
{\bm{\beta}}^{\top}_{*}{\bm{u}}=0
.

(a)

Under operator norm scaling (
\theta^{2}=c\tau^{2}
) with
\alpha_{Z}=1
,
\alpha_{A}=2
, alignment initially improves generalization for small
c
, but becomes harmful beyond a critical point, leading to catastrophic overfitting.

(b)

Under Frobenius norm scaling (
\theta=\sqrt{d}\tau
) with
\alpha_{A}=1
and
\alpha_{Z}=1.1
, alignment remains better than anti-alignment across all
c
, but benign overfitting is not achieved unless
\alpha_{Z}=\alpha_{A}
.

Figure 2
:
Transition from beneficial to harmful alignment under mild misspecification.
The scatter plots are empirically obtained and the lines are theory.

Equal Operator Norm Case.

For
\theta^{2}=\gamma\tau^{2}
, the excess risk is

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\begin{cases}\tau^{2}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\,\frac{\Delta_{1}^{2}}{1-c}\,\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}+\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\frac{c}{1-c}&c<1\\
\tau^{2}\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+c}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\Delta_{c}^{2}\left[\left(\frac{c^{2}+\gamma}{\gamma+c}\,\frac{c}{c-1}\right)-2\frac{\alpha_{A}}{\Delta_{c}}\right]+\alpha_{A}^{2}\tau^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{c}\right)+\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{c-1}&c>1\end{cases}

For
c<1
, the spike is
detrimental
. For
c>1
, the behavior depends on
\alpha_{Z}/\alpha_{A}
. In particular, if

\frac{1}{c}\leq\frac{\alpha_{Z}}{\alpha_{A}}\leq\frac{1}{c}\left(\frac{3c^{2}-\gamma+2c\gamma-2c}{(c^{2}+\gamma)}\right),

then we have that the coefficient in front of
({\bm{\beta}}^{\top}_{*}{\bm{u}})^{2}
is negative. Thus, when
\alpha_{Z}/\alpha_{A}
lies between these thresholds, the spike
helps
, but the spike
is harmful
outside this range. As
c\to\infty
, if
\gamma=o_{c}(c^{2})
, the beneficial region shrinks and
alignment increasingly harms generalization
. On the other hand, if the spike is big enough (
\gamma=\omega_{c}(c^{2})
), we have that the beneficial region limits to
0\leq\frac{\alpha_{Z}}{\alpha_{A}}\leq 2
. Figures
3(a)
and
3(b)
plot the coefficient of
({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}
for
c=2
and
c=20
for
\gamma=c
.

The upper bound on beneficial
\alpha_{Z}/\alpha_{A}
is surprising, as stronger target dependence on the spike might be expected to always favor alignment. Additionally, the dependence on the level of overparameterization
c
also offers new insights. Consider the example of
\gamma=c
, and
\alpha_{Z}/\alpha_{A}=2
. Then when
c<2
or
c>(9+\sqrt{57})/2
, we have that the ratio is outside the beneficial region. Figure
2(a)
shows that in the beneficial region, the aligned risk is lower than the anti-aligned risk. However, outside the beneficial region, the aligned risk becomes strictly larger than the anti-aligned counterpart.

Next, in terms of benign vs. tempered vs. catastrophic overfitting, we have that

\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{c}=\begin{cases}\tau^{2}\left[\gamma\alpha_{Z}^{2}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}+\alpha_{A}^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}\right]&{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\not\perp{\bm{u}},\gamma=\Theta_{c}(1)\\[5.0pt]
\infty&{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\not\perp{\bm{u}},\omega_{c}(1)\leq\gamma\leq o_{c}(c^{2})\\[5.0pt]
\tau^{2}\left[\alpha_{A}^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}+\left(\alpha_{Z}^{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{\phi}\right)-2\alpha_{Z}\alpha_{A}\right)({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\right]&{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\not\perp{\bm{u}},\gamma=\phi c^{2}\\[5.0pt]
\tau^{2}(\alpha_{A}^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}+(\alpha_{Z}^{2}-2\alpha_{Z}\alpha_{A})({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2})&{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\not\perp{\bm{u}},\gamma=\omega_{c}(c^{2})\\[5.0pt]
\alpha_{A}^{2}\tau^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}&{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\perp{\bm{u}}\end{cases}.

For
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\not\perp{\bm{u}}
, if
\omega_{c}(1)\leq\gamma\leq o_{c}(c^{2})
we have
catastrophic overfitting
. If
\gamma=\Theta_{c}(c^{2})
, overfitting is tempered, with benign overfitting precluded (Appendix
3
). If
\gamma=\omega_{c}(c^{2})
, overfitting is again tempered with benign requiring returning to the well-specified case (
\alpha_{A}=\alpha_{Z}
).

Equal Frobenius Norm Case.

For
\theta^{2}=d\tau^{2}
, the excess risk becomes:

\mathcal{R}_{c>1}=\alpha_{A}^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{c}\right)+({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\left[\frac{c}{c-1}\left(\alpha_{Z}-\frac{\alpha_{A}}{c}\right)^{2}-2\alpha_{A}\left(\alpha_{Z}-\frac{\alpha_{A}}{c}\right)\right]+\frac{\tau_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{c-1}.

For
c>1
, the beneficial region for the ratio
\alpha_{Z}/\alpha_{A}
is defined by:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\leq\frac{\alpha_{Z}}{\alpha_{A}}\leq 2-\frac{1}{c}.

The beneficial region expands with
c
, making alignment increasingly beneficial in extreme overparameterization (Figure
3(c)
). Beneficial alignment can also be seen in
Figure

2(b)
. Here
\alpha_{Z}/\alpha_{A}=1.1
, which is in the beneficial region for
c>10/9
. Finally, the overfitting is tempered unless
\alpha_{A}=\alpha_{Z}
.

(a)

Operator norm scaling
,
c=2
. Large beneficial region.

(b)

Operator norm scaling
,
c=20
. Smaller beneficial region

(c)

Frobenius norm scaling
,
c=1000
. The beneficial region persists at extreme overparameterization.

Figure 3
:

Phase boundaries for spike alignment impact.
Coefficient of
({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}
as a function of
\alpha_{Z}/\alpha_{A}
, indicating whether alignment improves or harms generalization.

3.3
Misspecified Target and Covariate Shift

Lastly, in addition to misspecifation, we also have covariate shift between train and test. Specifically,
\alpha_{Z}\neq\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}
or
\alpha_{A}\neq\tilde{\alpha}_{A}
, hence we have the spike/noise importance differ between train and test. For the
equal operator norm
case, we show the following.

Theorem 3
.

Given data
{\bm{Z}},\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}
that satisfy
1
,
{\bm{A}},\tilde{{\bm{A}}}
that satisfy
2
and
{\bm{y}},\tilde{{\bm{y}}}
according to
Equation

2
. If
3
holds, catastrophic overfitting occurs if
\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}=\alpha_{Z}
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\not\perp{\bm{u}}
, and
\omega_{c}(1)\leq\gamma\leq o_{c}(c^{2})
. Additionally, if
\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}\neq\alpha_{Z}
with
\gamma=\omega_{c}(1)
and
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\not\perp{\bm{u}}
we get catastrophic overfitting. Other scenarios yield tempered overfitting.

Different covariate shifts pose varying challenges. In particular, if
\alpha_{Z}\neq\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}
, (target’s spike dependence shifts), then catastrophic overfitting becomes unavoidable for sufficiently large spikes. This contradicts the earlier theoretical intuition, as increasing the spike size in this setting actually induces catastrophic overfitting instead of mitigating it.

Equal Frobenius Norm.

In this case, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 4
.

Let
{\bm{Z}},\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}
satisfy
1
,
{\bm{A}},\tilde{{\bm{A}}}
satisfy
2
and
{\bm{y}},\tilde{{\bm{y}}}
according to
Equation

2
. If
3
holds and
\alpha_{Z}\neq\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}
then
\mathcal{R}_{c}=\infty
for all
c\neq 1
.
For
\alpha_{Z}=\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}
:

\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{c}=\tau^{2}\left[({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}(\alpha_{Z}^{2}-2\tilde{\alpha}_{A}\alpha_{Z})+\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{A}^{2}\right].

If
\alpha_{Z}\neq\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}
, catastrophic overfitting occurs. When
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
and
{\bm{u}}
are parallel, we have that

\tau^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}(\alpha_{Z}-\tilde{\alpha}_{A})^{2}.

This is benign if and only if
\alpha_{Z}=\tilde{\alpha}_{A}
. Notably, if training data is misspecified (
\alpha_{A}\neq\alpha_{Z}
) but test data is well-specified and matches the training spike dependence (
\alpha_{Z}=\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}=\tilde{\alpha}_{A}
), benign overfitting becomes achievable.

3.4
General Theorem

Prior results are special cases of our main theorem (
Theorem

5
). Its full form is complex (Appendix
D
). We present a high-level decomposition here.

Theorem 5

(Generalization Risk)
.

Suppose
1
,
2
, and
3
hold.

\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{E}\left[\underbrace{\left\|\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}_{\text{Bias}}+\underbrace{\tau^{2}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}_{\text{Variance}}+\underbrace{\tilde{\alpha}_{A}^{2}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}_{\text{Data Noise}}+\underbrace{\left(-2\tilde{\alpha}_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\right)}_{\text{Target Alignment}}\right].

•

Bias.

This is the squared error between the learned predictor
{\bm{\beta}}_{int}
and the true parameter
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}

projected onto the spike direction

{\bm{u}}
.
In particular, the risk penalizes discrepancies only along the top eigen-direction of the population covariance
\Sigma
, reflecting the anistropic influence of the spike.

•

Variance.

The variance is equivalent to
\tau^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\|_{2}
.
This mirrors classical isotropic regression results
(
hastie2022surprises
;
bartlett2020benign
)
, but the norm
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\|^{2}
itself is dependent upon the interaction between signal and noise, the alignment between
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
and
{\bm{u}}
, and the scaling parameters.

•

Data Noise.

The data noise term quantifies the contribution of the noise matrix
{\bm{A}}
to the target outputs
y_{i}
through
\alpha_{A}
.
Even in the absence of observation noise (
\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}=0
), target corruption via data noise can create an irreducible error floor.

•

Target Alignment.

The alignment term measures the inner product between
{\bm{\beta}}_{int}
and
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
with respect to the sample noise covariance.
This cross-term captures how mismatch between
{\bm{\beta}}_{int}
and
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
, especially when mediated by
{\bm{A}}
, can amplify or dampen generalization error.

3.5
Extension: Nonlinear Models Also Exhibit Alignment Phase Transitions

While our theoretical focus is on linear regression, key phenomena like
\alpha_{Z}
dependent non-monotonic alignment effects appear in nonlinear models as well. We test this by training 3-layer ReLU networks to predict
{\bm{y}}
(
Equation

2
) given
{\bm{X}}
, where we vary the alignment angle between spike
{\bm{u}}
and
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
and record the generalization error. Figure
4
, shows our results for three
\alpha_{Z}
values. For
\alpha_{Z}=0.1
, increasing alignment with the spike is detrimental. For
\alpha_{Z}=1
, alignment is beneficial, while for
\alpha_{Z}=10
, alignment is detrimental again. This mirrors our theoretical findings that there is a region for beneficial alignment and a nuanced phase transition for different
\alpha_{Z}
values.

(a)

\alpha_{Z}=0.1
, alignment helps.

(b)

\alpha_{Z}=1
, mixed behavior.

(c)

\alpha_{Z}=4
, alignment hurts.

Figure 4
:

Alignment-phase transitions persist in deep networks.

Generalization error vs. angle between spike direction
{\bm{u}}
and ground-truth parameter
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
when fitting data with a 3-layer ReLU networks. The effect of alignment switches as
\alpha_{Z}
increases, consistent with the phase transitions predicted by our theory. Experimental details are in Appendix
B
.

4
Conclusion

This work provided a precise analytical characterization of the generalization error for minimum-norm interpolators in spiked covariance models. We decomposed the risk into interpretable components and comprehensively classified overfitting regimes based on spike strength, target alignment, and overparameterization. We reveal surprising phenomena, such as the potential for increasing spike strength to induce catastrophic overfitting before benign overfitting in well-specified aligned problems, and that strong target-spike alignment is not universally beneficial, especially under model misspecification. These alignment-dependent phase transitions, theoretically derived for linear models, were also empirically observed in nonlinear neural networks, suggesting broader relevance. Our results offer a more nuanced understanding of generalization in the presence of data anisotropy, challenging conventional intuitions and providing a detailed map of risk behaviors in overparameterized settings.

Other Notations.

We use lowercase
a
, lowercase bold
{\bm{a}}
, and uppercase bold
{\bm{A}}
letters to denote scalars, vectors, and matrices respectively. We use
\|\cdot\|_{2}
to denote the Euclidean norm if the argument is a vector and the operator norm if the argument is a matrix. We use
\|\cdot\|_{F}
to denote the Frobenius norm. When slicing one entry from a vector or matrix, we use both
a_{i}
,
A_{ij}
and
{\bm{a}}_{i}
,
{\bm{A}}_{ij}
, where the latter intends to emphasize the source of the scalar.

Appendix B
Non-Linear Experiment

We used 500 data points in 750 dimensional space, with a hidden width of 1000. We used full batch gradient descent for 100 epochs with a learning rate of 1e-4. Each data point is averaged over 50 trials. Equal Frobenius norm scaling was used for the size of the spike.

Appendix C
Spike Recovery Case

We consider the special case where the goal is to recover the spike direction
{\bm{u}}
. In this setting, the target
{\bm{y}}
depends only on the spike component
{\bm{Z}}
, with no contribution from the noise
{\bm{A}}
:

\alpha_{A}=\tilde{\alpha}_{A}=0,\qquad\alpha_{Z}=\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}=\alpha>0.

Thus, the target
{\bm{y}}
is proportional to the signal
{\bm{Z}}
plus possible observation noise
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
.

Equal Operator Norm

In this regime, we have that the risk is

\mathcal{R}_{c<1}=\frac{\gamma\alpha_{Z}^{2}\tau^{2}}{(1-c)(\gamma+1)}({\bm{\beta}}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}+\frac{c}{1-c}\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{c>1}=\frac{\gamma c(c^{2}+\gamma)\alpha_{Z}^{2}\tau^{2}}{(c-1)(\gamma+c)^{2}}({\bm{\beta}}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}+\frac{1}{c-1}\tau_{\varepsilon}^{2}

Here again, we see that when
\gamma=\Theta_{c}(1)
, we have tempered overfitting and
\omega_{c}(1)\leq\gamma\leq o_{c}(c^{2})
, we have catastrophic overfitting and for
\gamma=\Omega_{c}(c^{2})
we get tempered overfitting again.

Equal Frobenius Norm

. In this regime, we have that

R_{c<1}=\frac{\alpha_{Z}^{2}\tau^{2}}{1-c}({\bm{\beta}}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}+\frac{c}{1-c}\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\quad R_{c>1}=\frac{c\alpha_{Z}^{2}\tau^{2}}{c-1}({\bm{\beta}}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}+\frac{1}{c-1}\tau_{\varepsilon}^{2}.

This generalizes the spike recovery setting studied in
[
32
]
, which assumed noiseless targets (
\tau_{\varepsilon}=0
) and the equal Frobenius norm scaling. Our formula allows for observation noise and thus captures the more realistic case where the target
y
itself contains randomness not aligned with the spike. Here we see that we have tempered overfitting unless
\tau^{2}=o(1)
, which is the case considered in
[
32
]
.

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
5

See
5

In particular, as
n,d\to\infty
with
d/n\to c\in(0,\infty)
, we have the following expressions for each term.

Bias:
For
c<1
, we have that the bias term is

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}^{2}\left[({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}-\alpha_{Z}+(\alpha_{Z}-\alpha_{A})+\frac{\tau^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}}\right)^{2}+\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\frac{c}{1-c}\frac{1}{d(\theta^{2}+\tau^{2})}\right].

If
c>1
, we that the bias term is

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}^{2}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}-\alpha_{Z}+\left(\alpha_{Z}-\frac{\alpha_{A}}{c}\right)\frac{\tau^{2}c}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c}\right)^{2}+\tilde{\theta}^{2}\left[\alpha_{A}^{2}\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}}{d}\frac{c-1}{c}\frac{\theta^{2}\tau^{2}c}{(\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c)^{2}}+\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\frac{c}{c-1}\frac{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}}{n(\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c)^{2}}\right].

Variance:
For
c<1
, we have that the variance term is

\displaystyle\alpha_{A}^{2}\tilde{\tau}^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}+\tilde{\tau}^{2}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\left[\frac{1}{1-c}\frac{\theta^{4}+\theta^{2}\tau^{2}c}{(\theta^{2}+\tau^{2})^{2}}\left(\alpha_{Z}-\alpha_{A}\right)^{2}+2\alpha_{A}(\alpha_{Z}-\alpha_{A})\frac{\theta^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle+\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\left[\frac{c}{1-c}-\frac{\theta^{2}}{d(\theta^{2}+\tau^{2})}\,\frac{c}{1-c}\right].

For
c>1
, we have that the variance term is

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha_{A}^{2}}{c}-\frac{\alpha_{A}^{2}}{d}\frac{\theta^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c}\right)+\tilde{\tau}^{2}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}\frac{c}{(c-1)}\frac{\theta^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c}\left(\alpha_{Z}-\frac{\alpha_{A}}{c}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\tau_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{c-1}-\frac{\theta^{2}}{d(\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c)}\frac{c}{c-1}\right).

Data Noise:
For all
c
, we have that

\tilde{\alpha}_{A}^{2}\tilde{\tau}^{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}.

Target Alignment:
For
c<1
, we have that the alignment term is

-2\tilde{\alpha}_{A}\tilde{\tau}^{2}\left((\alpha_{Z}-\alpha_{A})\frac{\theta^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}+\alpha_{A}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}\right).

For
c>1
, we have that the alignment term is

-2\tilde{\alpha}_{A}\tilde{\tau}^{2}\left(\left(\alpha_{Z}-\frac{\alpha_{A}}{c}\right)\frac{\theta^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{2}+\alpha_{A}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}\left(\frac{1}{c}-\frac{1}{d}\,\frac{\theta^{2}}{\theta^{2}+\tau^{2}c}\right)\right).

Error terms:
The largest error terms for all
c
are:

o(1)+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)=o(1).

Remark:

We note that the above theorem is very general and captures all of the theorems in the main text as special cases. It is worth noting that the theorem also incorporates different signal and bulk strengths for test data, namely for
\tilde{\theta}
and
\tilde{\tau}
.

The proof will be broken up into roughly 6 steps

1.

Rescale the problem
To apply standard results we rescale the problem. Section
D.1

2.

Decompose the error into four terms.
We shall refer to these terms as the 1) bias, 2) variance, 3) data noise, and 4) target alignment. Section
D.2

3.

Simplify the expressions.
We shall then use the result from
[
25
]
to simplify the expression for each of the four terms. In particular, we shall express each term as the product of dependent functions of the eigenvalues of
{\bm{X}}
. Section
D.3

4.

Random matrix theory estimate.
We then use standard results from random matrix theory such as
[
22
,
3
,
5
]
to obtain a closed-form formula of the building blocks for the risk. Section
D.4

5.

Bound Products
. We then show that products of our building blocks concentrate. Step 4 (Section
D.5
) then collects the final terms.

6.

Undo Scaling
Step 5 (Section
D.6
) gives us back the correct scaling.

Section
E
has some generic probability lemmas that we need.

D.1
Step 0: Rescaling

In order to better align with existing results and use them accordingly, we change our scalings for now and switch back after our derivation. That is, we divide everything by
\sqrt{d}
. Hence, we shall use

\frac{\theta}{\sqrt{d}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{w}}^{\top}=\theta\frac{\|{\bm{w}}\|}{\sqrt{d}}{\bm{u}}\frac{{\bm{w}}^{\top}}{\|{\bm{w}}\|}

as the spike. We shall let

\eta^{2}:=\theta^{2}\frac{\|{\bm{w}}\|^{2}}{d}\quad\text{ and }\quad{\bm{v}}:=\frac{{\bm{w}}^{\top}}{\|{\bm{w}}\|}

Here, we treat
{\bm{v}}
as fixed unit norm vector and our spike is

{\bm{Z}}_{r}:=\eta{\bm{u}}{\bm{v}}^{T}

The
{\bm{A}}
noise after dividing by
\sqrt{d}
is

{\bm{A}}_{r}:=\frac{\tau}{\sqrt{d}}N

where
N
are mean zero variance 1 entries. Here the appendix, we shall use the letter
\rho
for
\tau
. Finally let

{\bm{X}}_{r}={\bm{Z}}_{r}+{\bm{A}}_{r}

We can note that
{\bm{\beta}}_{int}
, is still the solution to

\left\|\frac{{\bm{y}}}{\sqrt{d}}-{\bm{\beta}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}_{r}\right\|^{2},\quad\text{where }\ \frac{{\bm{y}}}{\sqrt{d}}={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{r}+{\bm{A}}_{r})+\frac{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}{\sqrt{d}}.

We define

\frac{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}{\sqrt{d}}=:{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{r}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}}{d}\right),\quad\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon,r}:=\frac{\tau^{2}_{\varepsilon}}{d}.

Then when we want to test, we shall look at the rescaled error

\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}(\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}_{r}+\tilde{\alpha}_{A}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}_{r})-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}(\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}_{r}+\tilde{{\bm{A}}}_{r})\right\|_{F}^{2}

Through Steps 1 - 4, we shall drop the subscript

\mathbf{r}
.

D.2
Step 1: Decompose Error

Using the fact that
\tilde{{\bm{A}}}
has been zero entries and is independent of
\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}
, we see that we can decompose the error as follows. Again here we consider
\tilde{n}
samples of test data and take the average (in expectation, this is the same as one test point).

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}(\tilde{\alpha}_{z}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\tilde{\alpha}_{A}\tilde{{\bm{A}}})-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}(\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\tilde{{\bm{A}}})\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\left\|\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\left\|\tilde{\alpha}_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\underbrace{\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\left\|\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}_{\text{Bias}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}_{\text{Variance}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\tilde{\alpha}_{A}^{2}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}}_{\text{Data Noise}}+\underbrace{\left(-\frac{2}{\tilde{n}}\tilde{\alpha}_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\right)}_{\text{Target Alignment}}\right].

We compute these four terms one by one in the following sections.

D.3
Step 2: Simplifying Terms

This section simplifies the four terms. We begin by recalling results from prior work. We state them here for completeness.

Theorem 6

(Theorems 3, 5 of
[
25
]
)
.

Define the following helper functions
{\bm{h}}={\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}
,
{\bm{k}}={\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}
,
{\bm{t}}={\bm{v}}^{\top}({\bm{I}}-{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{A}})
,
\xi=1+\eta{\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}
,
{\bm{s}}=({\bm{I}}-{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}){\bm{u}}
,
\gamma_{1}=\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2}
,
\gamma_{2}=\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2}
and

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}_{1}=-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}-\eta{\bm{k}},

\displaystyle{\bm{q}}_{1}^{\top}=-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}-{\bm{h}}.

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}_{2}=-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}-\eta{\bm{k}},

\displaystyle{\bm{q}}_{2}^{\top}=-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-{\bm{h}},

Then we have that

({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}=\begin{cases}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{p}}_{1}{\bm{q}}_{1}^{\top},\quad c<1\\
{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{p}}_{2}{\bm{q}}_{2}^{\top},\quad c>1\end{cases}.

The following subsections - Bias
D.3.1
, Variance
D.3.2
, Data Noise
D.3.3
, and Target Alignment
D.3.4
- present the linear algebraic simplifications of the results. To derive this results. We shall need some helper results that are presented in Section
D.3.5
.

D.3.1
Bias

Using
Lemma

5
, we have that if
c<1

\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=\left[\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}-\alpha_{Z}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}(\alpha_{Z}-\alpha_{A})\right]{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top},

and if
c>1

\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left[(\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}-\alpha_{Z}){\bm{I}}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}(\alpha_{Z}{\bm{I}}-\alpha_{A}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger})\right]\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-\alpha_{A}\frac{\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}.

The bias equals the expected squared norm of this term (divided by
\tilde{n}
).

D.3.2
Variance

Lemma
8
gives us that

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}\alpha_{z}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{Z}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}\alpha_{A}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{2\tilde{\tau}^{2}\alpha_{A}\alpha_{z}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{\varepsilon}}\right].

D.3.3
Data Noise

The data noise term is the simplest to understand. Preliminary calculation gives us:

\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\tilde{\alpha}_{A}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{A}}\left[\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{A}^{2}}{\tilde{n}}\frac{\tilde{\rho}^{2}\tilde{n}}{d}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{A}^{2}\tilde{\rho}^{2}}{d}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|^{2}.

D.3.4
Target Alignment

To understand this term, we first note that
\tilde{{\bm{A}}}
is independent of everything else. Hence we replace
\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}^{\top}
with its expectation
\frac{\tilde{\rho}^{2}\tilde{n}}{d}{\bm{I}}
.

\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{A}}\left[-\frac{2}{\tilde{n}}\tilde{\alpha}_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\right]=-\frac{2}{\tilde{n}}\frac{\tilde{\rho}^{2}\tilde{n}}{d}\tilde{\alpha}_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{int}=-\frac{2\tilde{\alpha}_{A}\tilde{\rho}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{int}.

Since
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
has mean-zero entries that are independent of everything else. We see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left((\alpha_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top})({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}+\alpha_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\right)^{\top}\right]

(5)

\displaystyle={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\alpha_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}-\alpha_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\right)^{\top}

(6)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{Z}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\alpha_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}.

(7)

D.3.5
Helper Lemmas

Proposition 1

(Proposition 2 from
[
32
]
)
.

In the setting from Section
2

{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}=\begin{cases}\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger},\quad c<1\\
\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{s}}^{\top},\quad c>1\end{cases}.

Lemma 1
.

If
\xi\neq 0
and
{\bm{A}}
has full rank, we have:

{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=\begin{cases}-\frac{\tilde{\eta}\xi}{\eta\gamma_{1}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}&c<1\\
-\frac{\tilde{\eta}\xi}{\eta\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}&c>1\end{cases}.

Proof.

After substitutions,
1
implies that for
c<1
,
{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}
becomes:

\displaystyle{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\left({\bm{A}}^{\dagger}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{p}}_{1}\left(-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}-{\bm{h}}\right)\right)\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle=\hskip 2.84544pt

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\left({\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{p}}_{1}\left(-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}-{\bm{h}}{\bm{u}}\right)\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)\quad\text{by $\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}$}.

Since
{\bm{k}}={\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}
and
{\bm{h}}{\bm{u}}={\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}=\frac{\xi-1}{\eta}
, we then have that

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\left({\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{p}}_{1}\left(-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}-{\bm{h}}{\bm{u}}\right)\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\hskip 2.84544pt

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\left({\bm{k}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\eta\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{p}}_{1}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2}-\xi}{\xi\eta}\right)\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\hskip 2.84544pt

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\left({\bm{k}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\eta\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{p}}_{1}\left(\frac{\gamma_{1}-{\xi}}{\eta}\right)\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\hskip 2.84544pt

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}+\eta{\bm{k}}\right)\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{1}{\eta}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\eta\gamma_{1}}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\hskip 2.84544pt

\displaystyle{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\left(-\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}-\frac{\tilde{\eta}\xi}{\eta\gamma_{1}}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\hskip 2.84544pt

\displaystyle-\frac{\tilde{\eta}\xi}{\eta\gamma_{1}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}.

For
c>1
, we note that the calculation is exactly the same. An example of such a calculation can be seen in the proof of
Lemma

4
.
∎

Lemma 2
.

In the setting of Section
2
, we have:

{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}=\begin{cases}{\bm{I}}-\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger},&c<1\\
{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}+\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{h}}-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}},&c>1\end{cases}.

Proof.

For
c<1
,
{\bm{Z}},{\bm{A}}
are
d\times n
with
d<n
. Since
{\bm{A}}
is assumed to have full rank,
{\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}}
has full rank with probability 1, and hence

({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}={\bm{I}}.

Thus, from
1
,

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}=({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}-{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}={\bm{I}}-\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}}-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}.

For
c>1
, since
({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}
is no longer the identity matrix, we directly expand using
Theorem

6
:

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle={\bm{A}}\left({\bm{A}}^{\dagger}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\eta{\bm{k}}\right)\left(\frac{\eta\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{s}}^{\top}+{\bm{h}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle={\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\eta{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\right)\left(\frac{\eta\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{s}}^{\top}+{\bm{h}}\right).

Noting that
{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}={\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger\top}{\bm{v}}={\bm{A}}^{\dagger\top}{\bm{v}}={\bm{h}}^{\top}
, we have

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle={\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\eta{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\right)\left(\frac{\eta\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{s}}^{\top}+{\bm{h}}\right)

\displaystyle={\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\eta^{3}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi\gamma_{2}}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{h}}-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}}.

We can combine the coefficients in front of
{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{s}}^{\top}
to get

\frac{\eta}{\xi}-\frac{\eta^{3}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi\gamma_{2}}=\frac{\eta(\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2})-\eta^{3}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi\gamma_{2}}=\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{2}}.

The statement follows from here.
∎

Lemma 3
.

If
\xi\neq 0
and
{\bm{A}}
has full rank, we have:

{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=\begin{cases}\left(1-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}\right){\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}&c<1\\
\left(1-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}\right){\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}&c>1\end{cases}.

Proof.

Using
1
for
c<1
and
\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}
, we have that

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}\right)\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right).

Note
\xi-1=\eta{\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}
,
{\bm{k}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}={\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{k}}=\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}
. The above equation becomes

\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\xi(\xi-1)}{\gamma_{1}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}\right){\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\xi(\xi-1)}{\gamma_{1}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}\right)\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}^{\top}.

Using
\gamma_{1}=\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2}
to combine the coefficients, we have that

\frac{\xi(\xi-1)}{\gamma_{1}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}=\frac{-\xi+\xi^{2}+\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}=\frac{-\xi+\gamma_{1}}{\gamma_{1}}=1-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}.

This completes the proof for
c<1
. Similarly, for
c>1
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{s}}^{\top}\right)\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{h}}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{s}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\eta\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{s}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right).

Note
\xi-1=\eta{\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}
,
{\bm{s}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}=\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}
. The above equation becomes

\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\xi(\xi-1)}{\gamma_{2}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}\right){\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\xi(\xi-1)}{\gamma_{2}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}\right)\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}^{\top}.

Using
\gamma_{2}=\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2}
to combine the coefficients, we have that

\frac{\xi(\xi-1)}{\gamma_{2}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}=\frac{-\xi+\xi^{2}+\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}=\frac{-\xi+\gamma_{2}}{\gamma_{2}}=1-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}.

The target expression follows.
∎

Lemma 4
.

If
\xi\neq 0
and
{\bm{A}}
has full rank, we have:

{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=\begin{cases}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}&c<1\\
\frac{\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}&c>1\end{cases}.

Proof.

We begin with
c<1
. Since
{\bm{A}}
is assumed to have full rank,
{\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}}
has full column rank with probability 1, and hence

({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}={\bm{I}}.

It follows from
Lemma

3
that

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-\left(1-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}\right){\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}.

For
c>1
,
{\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}}
now has full row rank instead of full column rank. Hence, we do not have
({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}={\bm{I}}
and need to directly expand it using
Theorem

6
and its helper variables:

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}\left({\bm{A}}^{\dagger}+\frac{\eta}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{p}}_{2}{\bm{q}}_{2}^{\top}\right)\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}\left({\bm{k}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{p}}_{2}{\bm{q}}_{2}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}\left(-\frac{1}{\eta}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{p}}_{2}\left(-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-{\bm{h}}\right){\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}\left(-\frac{1}{\eta}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{p}}_{2}\left(\frac{\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}+\frac{\xi-1}{\eta}\right)\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}\left(-\frac{1}{\eta}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{p}}_{2}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2}-\xi}{\xi\eta}\right)\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}\left(-\frac{1}{\eta}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{p}}_{2}\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}-\xi}{\xi\eta}\right)\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}\left(-\frac{1}{\eta}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{1}{\eta}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}-\frac{\xi}{\eta\gamma_{2}}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\tilde{\eta}\xi}{\eta\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\eta}\xi}{\eta\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\eta{\bm{A}}{\bm{k}}\right)\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}\quad\text{by plugging in the expression of ${\bm{p}}_{2}$}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\eta}\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}\quad\text{by $\tilde{\eta}{\bm{k}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}={\bm{A}}^{\dagger}\tilde{\eta}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}={\bm{A}}^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}$}.

Noting that
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}
is a scalar, we then introduce
1={\bm{u}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}
and get that

\frac{\tilde{\eta}\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}=\frac{\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}\quad\text{since $\tilde{\eta}{\bm{u}}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}=\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}$}.

Thus, the final expression is

\frac{\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}.

∎

Lemma 5

(Bias Term)
.

In the setting of
Section

2
, we have that if
c<1
,

\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}=\left[\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}-\alpha_{Z}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}(\alpha_{Z}-\alpha_{A})\right]{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top},

and if
c>1
,

\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}={\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left[(\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}-\alpha_{Z}){\bm{I}}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}(\alpha_{Z}{\bm{I}}-\alpha_{A}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger})\right]\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-\alpha_{A}\frac{\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}.

Proof.

To simplify the bias term, we first need the following expansion:

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}(\alpha_{z}{\bm{Z}}+\alpha_{A}{\bm{A}})+{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top})({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-\alpha_{z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}-\alpha_{A}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}.

From Lemmas
1
,
3
,
4
, we get simplified expressions for
{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}
and plug them in. For
c<1
, we get

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-\alpha_{Z}\left(1-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}\right){\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-\alpha_{A}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}-\alpha_{Z}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}(\alpha_{Z}-\alpha_{A})\right]{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{1}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{1}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}.

On the other hand, for
c>1
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-\alpha_{Z}\left(1-\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}\right){\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-\alpha_{A}\left[\frac{\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}\right]+\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}\left[(\tilde{\alpha}_{Z}-\alpha_{Z}){\bm{I}}+\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}(\alpha_{Z}{\bm{I}}-\alpha_{A}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger})\right]\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}-\alpha_{A}\frac{\eta\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{u}}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{Z}}}+\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\eta}\frac{\xi}{\gamma_{2}}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}{\bm{p}}_{2}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}^{\top}.

∎

Lemma 6

(Squared Norms of
{\bm{p}}_{1}
and
{\bm{p}}_{2}
)
.

Recall
{\bm{p}}_{1}=-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}-\eta{\bm{k}}
and
{\bm{p}}_{2}=-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}-\eta{\bm{k}}
.

1.

\displaystyle\|{\bm{p}}_{1}\|^{2}=\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi^{2}}\gamma_{1}.

2.

\|{\bm{p}}_{2}\|^{2}=\frac{\eta^{4}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{4}}{\xi^{2}}{\bm{h}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\frac{2\eta^{3}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}
.

Proof.

For
{\bm{p}}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\bm{p}}_{1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{t}}-\eta{\bm{k}}\right)\left(-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}-\eta{\bm{k}}^{\top}\right)=\left(\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}\right)^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}+2\frac{\eta^{3}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{t}}{\bm{k}}+\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}.

Using
{\bm{t}}{\bm{k}}=\bm{0}
yields the first result, which we can further simplify as

\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi^{2}}\left(\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2}\right)=\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi^{2}}\gamma_{1}.

For
{\bm{p}}_{2}
, similarly, we have

\displaystyle\|{\bm{p}}_{2}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{h}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger\top}-\eta{\bm{k}}^{\top}\right)\left(-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}-\eta{\bm{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{4}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{4}}{\xi^{2}}{\bm{h}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\frac{2\eta^{3}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}.

∎

Lemma 7

(Squared Norms of
{\bm{q}}_{1}
and
{\bm{q}}_{2}
)
.

Let
{\bm{q}}_{1}^{\top}=-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}-{\bm{h}}
and
{\bm{q}}_{2}^{\top}=-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-{\bm{h}}
.

1.

\displaystyle\|{\bm{q}}_{1}\|^{2}=\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{4}}{\xi^{2}}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger\top}{\bm{k}}+\frac{2\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}.

2.

\|{\bm{q}}_{2}\|^{2}=\frac{\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi^{2}}\gamma_{2}.

Proof.

Similar to Lemma
6
, we directly expand the two terms:

\displaystyle\|{\bm{q}}_{1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}-{\bm{h}}\right)\left(-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger\top}{\bm{k}}-{\bm{h}}^{\top}\right)=\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{4}}{\xi^{2}}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger\top}{\bm{k}}+\frac{2\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}+\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}.

\displaystyle\|{\bm{q}}_{2}\|^{2}=\left(-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-{\bm{h}}\right)\left(-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{s}}-{\bm{h}}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{4}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi^{2}}+\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}\quad\text{since ${\bm{h}}{\bm{s}}=\bm{0}$}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}(\eta^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2})}{\xi^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi^{2}}\gamma_{2}.

∎

Lemma 8

(Preliminary Expansion of Variance)
.

In the setting of
Section

2
, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}\alpha_{z}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{Z}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}\alpha_{A}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{2\tilde{\tau}^{2}\alpha_{A}\alpha_{z}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{\varepsilon}}\right].

Proof.

Since
\tilde{{\bm{A}}}
is independent of the other terms, we replace
\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}^{\top}
with its expectation
\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}\tilde{n}}{d}{\bm{I}}
.

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\left\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}\tilde{{\bm{A}}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\right]=\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}\tilde{n}}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{\beta}}_{int}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\right]=\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\|^{2}\right].

We now plug in the expression for
{\bm{\beta}}_{int}
. Since
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
is a zero-mean vector and independent from other random variables, terms with only one
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
have zero expectation. A straightforward expansion gives:

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}}{d}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}}{d}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}(\alpha_{z}{\bm{Z}}+\alpha_{A}{\bm{A}})+{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top})({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}(\alpha_{z}{\bm{Z}}+\alpha_{A}{\bm{A}})+{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top})^{\top}.

After eliminating zero expectations as above, the expectation becomes:

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}}{d}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{int}\|_{F}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}\alpha_{z}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{Z}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}\alpha_{A}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{A}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{2\tilde{\tau}^{2}\alpha_{A}\alpha_{z}}{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{2}}{d}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger}({\bm{Z}}+{\bm{A}})^{\dagger\top}{\bm{\varepsilon}}\right].

∎

D.4
Step 3: Random Matrix Theory Estimates

To do the estimates we recall the set up. In particular, we have that

{\bm{Z}}=\eta{\bm{u}}{\bm{v}}^{\top},\quad\text{where $\theta=\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{n}}$ and $\|{\bm{v}}\|=1$},

and the entries of

A_{ij}=\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\rho^{2}}{d}\right)

Recall the following definition
{\bm{h}}={\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}
,
{\bm{k}}={\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}
,
{\bm{t}}={\bm{v}}^{\top}({\bm{I}}-{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{A}})
,
\xi=1+\eta{\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{u}}
,
{\bm{s}}=({\bm{I}}-{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}){\bm{u}}
,
\gamma_{1}=\eta^{2}\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2}
,
\gamma_{2}=\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}+\xi^{2}
and

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}_{1}=-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{t}}^{\top}-\eta{\bm{k}},

\displaystyle{\bm{q}}_{1}^{\top}=-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{k}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}-{\bm{h}}.

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}_{2}=-\frac{\eta^{2}\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{A}}^{\dagger}{\bm{h}}^{\top}-\eta{\bm{k}},

\displaystyle{\bm{q}}_{2}^{\top}=-\frac{\eta\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}}{\xi}{\bm{s}}^{\top}-{\bm{h}},

To show that each of the four terms, bias, variance, data noise, and target alignment concentrate in the limit, we do this in two steps.

(a)

First, we compute the mean and variance for basic building blocks such as
\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}
and other variables. Section
D.4.1
.

(b)

Second, we provide bounds on the higher moments. Section
D.4.2
.

(c)

Next, we prove bounds on the moments of
\gamma_{i}
. Section
D.4.3
.

D.4.1
Step 3(a): Showing that basic building blocks concentrate

We begin by bounding the mean and variance.

Lemma 9

(Generalized version of Lemma 7 from
[
32
]
)
.

Suppose
A_{ij}
have mean 0 and variance
\rho^{2}/d
, the entries are uncorrelated, have finite fourth moment, the distribution is invariant under left and right orthogonal transformation and the empirical spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}{\bm{A}}{\bm{A}}^{\top}
converges to the Marchenko-Pastur law. Additionally, if
{\bm{u}}
and
{\bm{v}}
are fixed unit norm vectors. Then we have that

1.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2}]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\frac{c^{2}}{1-c}&c<1\\
\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\frac{c}{c-1}&c>1\end{cases}+o\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\right)
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\|{\bm{h}}\|^{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{4}n}\right).

2.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}]=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\frac{c}{1-c}+o\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\right)
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{4}n}\right).

3.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2}]=1-\frac{1}{c}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\|{\bm{s}}\|^{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{d}\right).

4.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2}]=1-c
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\|{\bm{t}}\|^{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

5.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\xi}{\eta}\right]=\frac{1}{\eta}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left(\frac{\xi}{\eta}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\max(n,d)}\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\right).

6.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\xi^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\right]=\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}+\frac{1}{\max(n,d)}\frac{c}{\rho^{2}|1-c|}+o\left(\frac{1}{\max(n,d)\rho^{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{\max(n,d)\rho^{2}}\right)
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left(\frac{\xi^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\max(d,n)\rho^{4}}\right).

Note that here
\max(d,n)
,
d
,
n
are interchangeable in the variance big-Oh terms since they only differ by an absolute constant
c
